     Balloc.ns
     Oeiiing Balloons and unlighted Pilot Balloons are used in daytime, Thceo
     balloons are about two f eet in diameter. Red or Whito oo1ours oro
     employed,
     At night, pilot balloons carry either a paper lantern lighted by a candle
     or A otnall battery-powered oleotr io light . These aro not likely t o be
     visibl e t o any gr eat height by the naked uye, but a balloon with a slow
     lao.k might carry a light acx·osa the sky for a considoro.blo distance at
     a r elatively low levol.
     Rodiosondo balloons are about fivo Ol' six f e<.t in diameter, coloured white,
     and carry a small box at the end of a cord about twenty f eet below th& balloon,
     Sometimes a radar r eflector 1e also t ied to the balloonJ this is o. r eflector
     of maey fac es and 1.t is possible that some unusual r eflections of light may occur
     from this attachment , Radiosonde balloons normally ascend to about 601 000 f eet,
     Skyhook Balloons are used occasionally for special high- altitude observations
     which takes them up to 100, 000 f eet , These balloons arc about 75 f eet
     in diameter and an instl'UJ'Ient box tied beneath, Bucauso of their size,
     thure is a good possibility t hat &~ch ball oons are the basis ot some reports
     of unusual aerial objects ,
     In the daytime with blue skies, good visibility and bright sunshine,
     balloons may bo seen at considerable heights once they aro l ocated by the eye,
     Tho balloons stand out against the blue of t he sky as sharp pin points of
     light ,
      Airornft
      Aircraft sec.n 1n this country should conform to the well known silhouettes,
      but, in view of d~volopments in neighbouring countries, triangular (delta
      wing) and tailless typos, possibly flying at great heights, 11\tly bo soon. Also
      due to distances and air craft attitudes in flight, the true plan f orma may
      not be obsorvod, A chango in shape during t he observation may well
      indicate the existence of these circumstances .
      Effects of Soroens, Glass , Etc ,
      common object s when viewed through screens may bu distorted out of all
      r eoognizablo shapes ; often single objects may appear ae sevaro.l , As
      commer • i nl sheet glass (window panes) may contain defects causing similar
      optical phenomena , observers Should be wary of such conditions,
      Nacreous or 11other of Pea;rl Clouds
      These r are cl ouds are most likely to be seen just before sunrise or just
      after sunset whon illuminated by sunshine trom below th~ horizon. n1ey
      M~ also ~ppoat· in daytime, The clouds occur at heights of l$ t o 20
      ~ilea and havu iridescent colour s which r esemble the colours seen in mother
      of pear l . lt is possible that a small detuched mother of pearl cloud might
      give tho nppear ance of a hovering obj llct high in the sky,
      Nootilucent 0 ouds
        oo ucen or n p, t - luminous clouds are seen only at night made visible by
      r eflect ed light from the sun when the sun is about 10° t~ 16° below the
      hor izon and visibility conditions are very good. They usun.lly appear
      about an hour after sunsotj l ow on the horizon, Their colour may be white
      or they m~ be a shade of colour suoh as bluish-white , gold~n , or reddish-
      or ange, but they do not display tho brilliant iridescent colours that are
      characteristics of mother of pearl clouds, Noctilucent clouds occur
      about SO miles high in the atmosphere, as determined by simultaneous
      photography £rom diffare~t points on the earth's surface . Their speeds
      havo boon caloulatud to be as high as 400 miles per hour, but beoause of
      their great hoight thoy appear to move slowly,

Clouds Reflections
Reflllctions ot light trom cloud bunks aro also a possible sourco of
r eports of illuminated objects in the sky at night, The source of
light may bo nny kind of a searchlij!ht, such as ceiling proj~;~ctors ,
defence units, aircraft landing- lights, ato . Usually the bewn of the
light is visible from the source up to illuminated spot, so this condition
is not likely to deceive a careful observur,
Optical Phenomena
Rainbows are common optical phenomena oausod by a r efraction and
reflection of light from the sun by VJa t er dt'Ops in the atmospher e .
A smill portion only of a r ainbOw may be soen at tilnes which ml.ght
give the appearance of a small obj11ct in tho sky, However, boonuao
r ainbOws ar e f airly oommon oocurrenc~:: , they are unlikely to deceive
anyono,
Optical phenomena causud by r~flections of li~ht from ice crystals
suspendod in the atmoephuro may r esult in tho appearance of unusunl lighting
effects in thu sky. The halo around the sun or moon is tho most oo~on of
this class of phenomenl\, -,no halo is usually seen as a ring of; 22° radius
around tho sun or moon, but under some conditions it is pocsibla for
only part of the ring to bo formed, Sundogs or mock- suns may appear
Rt <~n angl u of 220 oitht'lr t o tho right or 1\)ft, of the sun • these appear
as bright spots of light in the sky. Mock· suns sometimes are seen at angles
of 46°or 90° from the sun, The possibility of halo phenomena should
always be considered when any br ight spot is seen in the sky - such
spots will r emain r elatively fixed in position, Halo phenomena are most
commonly caused by the ~1n bucauso of tho large 11mount of light available
from this source:, but the complet e hnlo r!ing is treqnently seen around t he
moon nt night and it is possible that unner unusual conditions other hal o
phenomono may also be seen at night.
Meteors
Am&teor, or shooting stnr, alwSfs pursues n nearly straight (or great
circle) path across t he sky. Faint meteors last about r~ a second,
brighter ones rar ely moru than two or three seconds, Bright meteors may
buret and shower sparks or may l eave a f aint luminescence in tholr wake
that is eome~ime visible for several minutes. Bright meteors may appear
of almost any colour and in exceptional oasos produce detonations and
rumbling sounds, When coming head on ~ moteor seems to have almost no
motion acr ose the sky but whon movi~ porpcndioulsr to the line of sight its
appBr ont velocity is r apid,
Stars and Planets
Stare and planets can general~ be r ecognized without diffio~lty but on cer·
tain occasi ons appear with unusual brilliance thus exciting ooMent, In
any oase they never movo rapidly but have A slow general motion from
the east to the west part of the sl<y, except for the stare in tho nor th lob ere
the;, motion ill counter-clookVJise about the polo star. Venus at il.ts gr eat est
brilliance can appear in the sunlit· sky as a faint 14hite dot visible
t o the nnkdd eye . It eit her pr ecedes or f ollows the sun on t hdse occasions.
    The Aurora
    Tho aurora, or northern light, produces various luminous f orms of nume.r ous
    pastel shades. In most parts of Canada northern lights mey appoar in any
    pal"t of the sky thou~h they are seen most froquontly in t ha north . Although
    sharp xnys may appear as port of the display most of the illum1n~t1on is of

  Part I - Information for guidance in reporting on unknown flying objeoto.

        In collecting data on unknown flying objects, aocura~ of
  observation and record i s of prime importance. The observer should
  report oarei'ully and precisel_y 'lltlat he sees and hears with a
  Rinimum of private personal interpretation. Accurate nurnerioal data
  to the beet of the observer ' s ability are most desirable. Confirmation
  of the observation by ethers is also desirable, particularly 1t other
  observers are located some distance away so t hat they mB:¥ have a slightly
  different view of t he object.
        The sighting r eport is for the purpose of obtaining specific infor-
  mation r egarding 11 particular sighting . Most of the questions are
  str aightf orward and call f or an obvious answer. So~e questions, however,
  may r equire a oortain amount of explanation so that the r equired information
  may be forthcoming . I t should be noted that tho information obtained will
  not be m.ade public.

  Part II • Descriptions of Normal Phenomena which might cause r eports
            or unidentified aerial objects.
  Balloons
  Celllng Balloons and unlighted Pilot Balloons aroused in daytime. These
  balloons nro about two f eet in diameter. Red or White colours are
  employed.
  At night, pilot balloons carry either a paper lnntern lighted by a candle
  or a small battery- powered oluotric light. Those oro not likely to be
  visible to any great height by the naked eye, but a balloon with a slow
  leak might carry a light across the sky for a considerable distance at
  a r elatively low levul.
  Radiosonde balloons are about five or six fe~t in diameter, coloured white,
  and carry a small box at t ho end of a cord about twenty feet below the balloon.
  Sometimes a r adar r eflector is alao tied t o the balloonJ this is a r eflector
  of many £acos and i t is possible that some unusual reflections of light may occur
  f r om this attachment . Radiosonde balloons nonnally ascend to about 60, 000 f eet .
  Skyhook Balloons are used oooasionally for special high-altitude observations
  which takas them up to 100,000 f eet. These balloons are about 75 feet
  in diameter and an instr ument box tied beneath. Boc:~use of their size 1
  thore is a good possibility that such balloons ar e the basis of somo r eport s
  of unusual Aerial objects.
  !11 tht~ daytime with blue skius 1 good visibility and bright sunshine,
  balloons may ba seen at considerable heights once thoy are l ocated by the eye,
  Tho balloons stand out agninst the blue of t ho sky as sharp pin points of
  light .
  Airore.rt.
  Alrcr art soon in t his country should conform to the well known silhouettes,
  but, in view of dev~lopmonts in neighbouring countries, triangular (dolta
  wing) and tailless types, possi-bly !'lying at great hE;ights, may bo soon. Also
  due t a distances and air cr aft attitudes in flight, the true plan f oms ma.y
  not be oboervod. A chango in shape during the observation may well
  indicate tho axietence o£ those circumstances ,

   Nacr eous or Hother of Po11rl Clouds
   These r are cl ouds are most likelY t o be seen Just bef or e sunrise or just
   after sunset when illuminated by sunshina from below t he horizon. Th&y

Dear 14r. Smith.:
     After our conversation concerning the mathematical
problem raised by your model of the electron in con-
nection with the fljing saucer, I had some discussions
or the problem with the men in my group here .                We come
to the conclusion that the mathemtical problem is a
very complicated one in th~ following sense:                 on starting
with the Maxwell equations i n wbioh the dielectric constant
and the magnetic permeability are fUnctions of the coordinates
as well as are tensors, one would get for the ~ave equation"
a very complicated partial differentiation oonta~ining
the derivatives or these constants as well as of the field
strengths .     It is not sufficient io know the fUnction ~
but must have the /''    and -x:   separately as lmown fUnctions
before one can proce4•.     Hence we have not gone any further
into the problem.
     I regret that we ha~e not been able to be or much
help to your problem, but I appre ciate your kindness in
discussing it with me.
                                     Yours sincere~,

                               For the benefit of those \7ho were not pre-
                     sent at the general meeting hel d on April 22nd , the
                     Chairman opened the meeting with s brief resume of
                     discussions leading to the formation of this Committee
                     and the terms of reference . He then tabled thr ee books
                     by Kehoe, Scully and Heard dealing with Flying Saucer s
                     sightings as wor thy study as they give a fairly useful
                     summary of the most important publioslly recor ded sight-
                     ings. These can be used in addition to newspaper reports .
                                S/L Bing tabled a r e port by the RCAF relative
                      to the USAF project on Flying Saucers. This report i s
                      to be duplicated by the RCAF and copi es passed to the
                      Secretary for distr ibution to the Committee members .
                                The question of the s eourity classification
                      of the work of the committee was then discussed . It
                      was agreed that CONFIOEtlTIAL was sufficient , but that
                      members should be cleared t o SECRET to f ac ilitate any
                      exchange of infor mat ion from international sources .
                                  The Cha1~man stated that it would probably
                      be better to divorce this work from the questionable
                      title of "Flyint Saucers", and thought thet some name
                      entirely without meaning such as 11 P~ojeot Theta " should
                      be adopted . The Committee agreed in principle to this
                      suggestion , with the e:xception that the project name
                      is not to be used during exchange of information of en
                      international cha racter. Such 11 exchenges 11 are to be
                      effected through established channels only . There being
                      no further suggestions acceptable , the name IIProjeot
                      Thete 11 was adopted, subj ect to investigat ion by tho
                      Secretary as to the validity of the use and possible
                      duplication of the na me ,
                                  The poss ibility of obtaining u.s . date on
                      a r ec iprocal ba s is was considered desirable end the
                      5eor etsry was instructed to have a formal approach to
                      this effect made through the DRB ~~ mber in Washington .
                                  Consideration was given to the int er rogation
                      form presentl y 1n use by the RCAF . After discussion ,
                      it was agreed tha t the form should immediately be re -
                      vised with accompanying instructions for its use . The
                      r evised form (oopy attached) was drawn up and copies
                      a re to be distributed by the Secretary prior to the
                      next meet i ng , The preparation of the instructions wa s
                      divided among the members as follows •

          1.         :Salloono.
           1.1.      Oailine Balloone and unlighted Pilot :Balloons are aced in doytime ,
          Thaee balloons ere aboat two feet in diameter. Red or white coloare 9re
          employed.
           1 . 2.    At ni ght , pilot balloons carry either a paper liUltern lighted by
          a candle or a small battery-powered electric light , Theee are not likely ~o
          be visible to any great height by the naked eye but a balloon wit h a elow
          leak might carry tho light acroas the sky for a ooneiderable distance at a
          relatively low level ,

           1.3.       Radiosonde llnlloons are about five or aix feet in diameter, oolournd
          white , und carry a small box at the end of a cord about twenty feet below the
          balloon . Sometimes n radnr reflector ia also tied to the balloon: thia ie
          a reflector of many faoea and i t i a poaeible that aome unueaal reflection•

                  DEFENCE RESE~RCH BOARD
                                           I


         Minutes of a Meeting to Discuss 11 Flying
         Saucers 11 Sighting, 22 April , 1952.

    Chairman a                Dr . o.u. Solandt(DRB)
    Present a                S/L L. P.S, Bing (JIB)
                             F/L V.L. Bradley (DRB)
                             Col. 0 . M. Carrie (ORB~
                             0/0 P. M. Edwards ~DAI
                             Mr . A. J . Langley DRB
                             Dr . P. M. Millman Dom. Observ .)
                             Cdr . J .C. Pratt    (DNI)
                             Mr . W.B. Smith (DOT)
                             Lt . Col . E.H . Webb (DMO & P)
    Secretary a               Mr . H. C, Oatway (ORB)
                         The Chairman opened the meeting with
    a brief reference to the more frequent occurence of
    11 Flying Seucer 11 sightings.  The frequency and persis-
    tency of the sightings would tend to discount the theory
    of 'hallucinations' . This , coupled with an aroused
    public interest in these sightings , tended to call for
    a more active stand on the matter . At present the
    gathering of reports was rather haphazard end ·the
    rea ction of the Services was passive . It i s thought
    that a more active and intensive effort should be made
    to obtain these data on an organized basis , and all
    r eports investigated and analysed . The objects of
    the meeting were then to determine if a more serious
    effort is justified and, i f so, \'ays end means of
    implementing an organized effort . Organizations such
    as the Observer Corps might be enlisted for the job
    of sighting . If nothing else, this could serve as a
    useful training for the Corps . An examination of the
    theories might prove useful in giving a lead to the
    best locations for sighting . It was fortunate that
    proponents of the th eories of terrestrial ahd extr a-
    terrestrial origins were in contact with the D.R.B.
    and their data could be examined first hand .
                     The Air Force representative s then
    briefly outlined the work done by the USAF up to a
    year or so ago . As these efforts consistentl y
    resulted in 'nil returns' their project, 1n so far
    as the press and public were concerned at l east , had
    been discontinued . Very recently , however , this in-
    vestigation was re - opened , but is now classified .
                      In the discussions which followed 1
    it was pointed out that precise and rea l istic detslls
    were lacking in all known reports . I f observer s such
    as the Rangers, watchers on ship board and the Obser-
    ver Corps , which incidentally is really still in the
    paper organization stage , are to be enlisted some
    well pl3nned guidance would be necessary . A small
    booklet illustrating typical celestial phenomena
    would result in more intel ligent observation and
    eliminate many errone ous impressions . It was con-
    sidered desirable to obtain information f r om u.s .
    interviews obtained under proper interrogation pro-
    cedure, but to avoid the u.s. analysis of these
                                      CONFIDENTIAL
                          - 2 -
interviews Vlhich was often unacceptabl e to some members
of this Committee ,
                 Mr . Smith briefly outlined the extra-
terrestrial orig i n theory , A plot of the frequency and
timing of sighting related to the opposition of the pla-
net Hers to the earth was displ ayed . Sighting a occur
at approxi~tely six week intervals but the f r equency
is much higher during periods when ihe planets are near-
ing each other such as in the present month . The more
r eliabl e observations pl ace these objects at heights
of 100 to 300 miles moving witn velocities in the order
of 1000 - 2000 mph . Terrestrial bodies making use of
airfo ils could not operate at these he ights. Size and
power limitations also negate earthy origins , The bri-
lliance of sighting after sunset could be explained by
r eflections from the body at these altitudes of the
sun's illumination , or in daylight by frictional heating
or other magnetic heating effects , Considering the
orbital velocities of the ear th and Mara ( 18 and 1 5
miles per secon respectively) and their nearest posi t i ons
(e.g . 52 1000,000 miles on May 8th) with a cont inuous
accelera tion of 2 g for 3 or 4 days , these distances
could be traversed ,
                Mr . Langley stated that no electronic
reports had been received of f l ying saucers . It was
generally agreed that no electromagnetic radiations had
ever been found which could not be traced to terrestrial
origin . If electronics are associated with these objects,
t heir frequencies are outside the presently usable ranges,
Mr. Smith then elaborated on the wor k of the ionosphere
stations which had been asked to report any unusual
findings, but with 'nil returns ' to date .
                The Chairman outlined in broad terms the
theory of terrestr ia.l origin , namely a new type of air-
oraft (presumably Russian) as expounded by Mr . Frost
of the A. V, Roe Company. This theory had some disore -
psncies , but the aerodynamics were worth following as,
even if of extra ~terrestrial origin the bodies would
have to follow aerodynamic theory wlthin the earth atmos-
phere . Also a new high speed aircraft design might be
e~ olved . The theories outlined should give an impetus
to the flying saucer investigations .
                It was generally agr eed that a more active
investigation should be undertaken, The Chairman believed
that the function of the D.R. B. should be mainly advisory
as the collecting of r eports could best be done by field
organizations . The r epresentatives of the Services agreed
and will use their discretion in the choice of their more
suitable sections to use for observers.
                  It was decided that a Committee should be
formed to give a lead in this activity and to standardize
procedures , etc . Accordingly the follewing were nominated
and agreed to ao~1 Dr . Millman (Chairman) G/C Edwards,
Lt . Col . v.ebb1 Cdr Pratt 1 F/L Bradley , and Mr . Oatway
(Secretary) This committee was to prepare a brief of
instructions for observers ; examine interrogation procedures
to get a consolidated end per tinent series of que stions ;
                        - 3 -       CONfiDENTIAL


and to establish a standard method of recording and
indexing for subsequent analysis.
             Finally G/C Edwards suggested that the
RCLW might prove to be a valuable addition as obser-
vers. This organization has the added advantage of
having trained interrogators.
             The Chairman thanked those present for
attending and for their willingness to aid in these
investigations .


26 April 1952
OTTAWA .

MEMORANDUM:·

    TO     or. J.R. G. Keyston
    A

                                 Request from Mr. Stephen Michalak
                Re your mc11o concerning the letter from Stephen Michalak, dated
           December 8, 1969, the original of the drawing he requests was not
           on the Michalak file when we received it from the Department of
           National Defence in March, 1968, nor was there any evidence that
           the original had ever been on the file. An 8 x 10 photographic print
           of the original drawing was on the file and thi.s print is stamped on
           the back wi th tho number WG 67348-2. There is also a clipping, taken
           from tho Winnipeg Tribune of Monday, May 22, 1967, with a picture of
           Nr. Michalak holdi.ng the original drawing.
                As you suggested in our phone conversation, we should contact
           other departments to ascertain whether they have knowledge of the
           location of the original of the drawing. The following were involved
           in the early invest.igation, commenced in May, 1967 ·
                W/C D.F.   Robertson, Ops. 4, Ottal!'a NDHQ/R.C.A. P.
                S/L P. Bissky, Traini,ng Colllllland H.Q., Westwin, Man.

                W.B. Cowie, ORB, Defence Chemical Biological and Radiation
                     Laboratories .                      ·
                S.B. Hunt, Department of National Health and Welfare,
                     Radlation Protection Division, Brookfield Rd., Ottowa 8, Ont.
                Cpl. J. Davis and Con.s t. J, Zackarais, R,C.M.P ., ClO , Winnipeg
                D. Thompson, Manitoba Health and Welfare Oepart11ent , Winnipeg •


                                                     . Peter M. Mill•an.

                Commissioner t1. F . A. Lindsay,
                Commissioner 's Office,
                Royal Canadian nuounted Pol1·ce HQ I
                1200 Alta Vi sta Drive ,
                Ottawa, Ontario
                Dear Commissioner Lindsay,
                          Enclosed are copies of recent correspondence
                concerning a drawing of an Unidentified Flying Object,
                the original of which was apparently given to some
                government official, and is now reclaimed by its artist ,
                Mr. Stephen Michalak.
                          Since your organization was involved in the
                investigation of this UFO, I am writing to ask if you
                could shed any light on the whereabouts of this drawing.

                                                      Yours sincerely ,


                                          ____,5?,-~:J
                                                    J,R, Keyston
                                           Executive Assistant to the President

           .,
          1OIJ(I
                    Stefan ~11CHALAK - Report' of Unidenti1'ied Flying Object
                    Falcon Beach , Manitoba        20 MAY 67

            1.     Reference to previous correspondence, attached are 11 coloured
            photos taken near to and at the site of the alleged U. F . O. landing .
            These photos are for furtherance to H.Q. Ottawa . The sighting by
            MICHALAK 1B reported to have happened near Falcon Beach, Manitoba on
            Saturday , May 20th , 1967 at about 12:15 P.M. These photographs were
            taken on July 28th, 1967, over 2 months after the alleged sighting.
            2.      The photos have been numbered l to ll inclusive on the reverse
            and following is what each picture illustrates:
             (1)       A view of the area on the rock void of moss and the investigators
                       removing radio- active earth samples .
             ( 2)      A closer view of the same scene showing a semi- circular area
                       allegedly made by the U. F . 0 .
             ~il
                       Similar to (2 ) showing personnel at the site .
                       Taken from where MICHALAK claims to have been standing when he
                       saw the U. F. O. land. In the distance our members can be seen
                       through the trees standing where the U.F . O. landed .
             ( 5)      A view taken furt her back similar to (4) but showing the rock
                       which MICHALAK was working on when he saw the U. F . O. In the
                       distance through the trees , members can be seen standing on the
                       landing spot .
             ( 6)      The view taken halfway between where MICHALAK first was when he
                       saw the U. F . O. and the actual landing spot .
             ( 7)      A view of the landing spot taken closer to the actual spot and
                       from along the path MICHALAK followed when he was approaching
                       the u.F.o.
             (8)       A view standing on the edge of the clearing where the U. F.O.
                       landed .
             (9)       A slide of the landing spot taken from part way up a tree .
                       Definite outline of a ci rcular moss-void area can be seen .
             H~~
                       Same as (9 ) with the area cleared of personnel .
                       Slide of MICHALAK i n the blue shirt with S/Leader BISSKY.
            These photographs were handed to me by Civilian Member A. R. CORRIGAN

        Introduction
                  On the ~4 July 67 the Physics Section of this Division monitored
        on behalf o~ the R.C . M.P . Crime Detection Laboratory, Ottawa, samples of
        soil , burnt shir t and steel tape for possible radioactive contamination .
        A gammaray spectral analysis of the 2 2~ree samples revealed activity levels
        of up to ap proximately 0 . 5~i of Ra     or its equivalent. The samples
        had been collected from the alleged landing site of a U. F. O. near Falcon
        Lake , Manitoba. The information receiv ed by this Division on the 24 and
        25 July 67 suppolt1ng this particu.l ar sighting and samples were vague
        and disjointed . Further light was shed on the matter when the undersigned
        had an opportunity to :review the investigation report of the U. F.O .
        sighting submitted by 8/L P. Bissky, (R.C. A.F. ) through the courtesy of
        Inspector Hendrick during a visit to the R. C. M. P . Crime Detection Lab-
        oratory, ottawa . A»Dendix A to this memorandum is a duplicate copy of
        S/L Bissky 1 s report .
               The task of assessing the radiation hazarO to the general public
        from the landing site did not initially indicate that it would be nec-
        essary forihis Divi s ion to involve itself in the R. C.M .P . and D. N.D .
        investigation . Rowever, it soon became obvious to the undersigned that
        collection and oo-ordination of all background information 11as essential
        due to the following •
                 (a)   The apparent lack of cooperation by the prinoi,p als towarO
                       the military and police.
                 (b)   The exact location of the sighting bad not been established
                       at the time o~ the Division's initial jnvolvement .
               The undersigned flew to Winni peg on the 26 July 1967 where he was
        met by Mr . D. Thompson of the Manitoba lieal th Department . Mr . Thompson
        advised the undersigned that a meeting had been arranged between l'epre-
        aentativea of the R.c. M.P. and D. N. D. and the Federal and Provincial
        Health rep1•esentat1ves . This meeting was to establish what would be
        the beet way to proceed .

Field Investigation Report
         26 July 1967, 1330 bra . - R.C . M. P. Headquarters Winnipeg- Met
with the Superintendent Shark , R.C. M. P., S/L Biaaky , R.C.A.F., Cpl . Davia,
R.C . M.P ., and Messr s . B. Rendall and D . Thompson of' the Manitoba Depart-
ment or He~lth . A briering of what had havpened to date was given by
S/L Bissky . This brie~ins was a very b r ief review of the investigation
report compiled by S/L Biaeky . A discussion fol lowed the briefing ae to
what was t he best way to proceed . The following actions were decided
upon .
         1 . Messrs . Thompson and Hunt to visit Mr . Earl Campbell of th~
              Manitoba Oa.n oer Clinic to ascertain the exact location of
              the Radi um burial ground at East Braintree .
             Messrs . Thompson and Bunt to vie1t !~land Cement Company to
             find out if the company has any Ra 2

        26 July 1967 , 143u hrs . -Messrs . Thompson and Hunt met with Mr , E.
Campbell or t he Mani t.oba Cancer Clinic. Mr . Campbell showed photographs
of the s ite and gave us the name of a Mr . R. Kemp who could guide us to
the site . During hie visit. we checked, fol' radioactivity, some samples
of soil and vegetat1 1.111 collected f'rom a Beausejour U. F.O. sighting. The
samples were checked on a scintillation counter . There was no evidence
of any radioactivity.
        26 July 1967, 1800 bra . - S/L Biasky , Cpl . Davie and Messrs .
Thompson and Hunt visit the r·eeidence of Mr . B. Michalak l ocated 314 Lind-
ea,y Street . An atten1.vt v.as made to persuade Michalak to take ut to the
site of' the U. F.O. landing, however , at the end of the visit it s t ill
appea1•ed unl1 kely that be woUld go as he f' el t t hat he would lose too
much money by missing a day s work . Mr . Michalak showed the undersigned
samples of rock , soils and vegetation collected from the U. F.O , site .
These samples were located in hie basement . A survey of hie basement
showed no evidence of' any loose contamination. Radiation fields were
dete4ted co~ing from the plastic bags containing soil and the remnants
of Michalak's burnt shirt . Michalak was at first unwilling to part with
the samples , but eventually allowed the undersigned to have part of the
samples from each bag fol' further analy sis . Mr. Michalak has no respect
for contamin2ted materials . He h~ndled the contaminated soil with his
bare handa , and was made to wash his hands with great difficulty . During
our conversation with Michalak he produced a aeries of' photographs taken
by Lite Magazine represent atives . These i ncluded photos of' the site and
colour photos of the burns on his abdomen and the ecorced f:1d pattern
on his undershirt . At the time of'this interview , Michalak s wife and
eldest eon were present .
        27 July 1967, 1000 hrs , - Users Thompson and Hunt visited the
Radium burial site st East Braintree (see Appendix B) . We were guided by
Mr . R. Kemp , Con'5'er r,latiotl Of:f'1oer for the Renewable Resources Branch ,
Department of Mines and Natural Resources for Manitoba . Only one of' the
ste el spikee ueed to mark the burial site could be located . However ,
P.~ Three
the area was easi ly recognizable from the photographs shown to us by
Mr. E. Campbell (Manitoba Cancer Cl j nic) . It appeared to us that the
soils had not been disturbed as roost of the area was covered with veg-
e tation. Another t wo years will see the area completely ovei•grown with
vegetation . No radiation checks were made as there was a minimum of
three feet of sand covering the contaminated materials . The maximum
amount of radium located at the burial site ie in the area ot 12 mgms .
          27 July 1967, 1200 bra . -Net S/L Bieeky, F/0 Smith, Cpls . Shepard
and Reed , all of t he R.c .A.F ., Cpl. Davie and Constable Zacharias of the
R. C. M. P ., and Mr. Michalak at the Falcon La~e Provincial Par k entrance .
After lunch , t he above mentioned plus Thompson and Hunt set out for the
u.F.o. landing s ite . This involved cr ossing to the north aide of Hwy.
No . l and following the route a a indicuted on the attached map . The en-
t ire expedition too~ about 45 minutes to reach its d~ ination . The site
was confirmed a s being~e correct site by Constable Zacharias who had been
taken to it by Mr. B. Thompson ot the Vlinnipeg Airborne Phenomena Research
Organization the night before . The landing area of the U.F. o . was re-
cognizable from the photographs taken by Life Magazine , the origin of
the c i~cular outline of vegetation in the wrlter's opinion i s debateable .
A thorough survey o~ the landing area was cal'r1ed out , usi ng a Tracerlab
SU14 , Admiral Radiac 5016, and a Civil Defence CDV 700 survey meter. One
small a~e a was found to be contruninated. This was located across the
crown of the rock . The~e was a smear of contamination about 0 . 5 x 8 . 0
inches on one side of the c r ack . There was alao some lichen and ground
vegetation contamJnated just beyond the smear . The whole contamlnated
area wae no larger tha.n 100 equaree inches . All water run o1.'f areas were
checked for possible contamination , but nothing was f ound. Samples of
the c ontaminated rock a nd lichen were taken for further analyaie . Photo-
graphs of the area wero t aken by Cple . Shephard and Davis and Mr . Thompson.
Since there wae no serious health hazard involved due to the remoteness
of the area and also due to the fact th.at the majority of the contamination
wae taken for samples , no :further precautions were felt necessary e. t the
U. F.O . site . Prior to returning to Winnipeg , it was decided that Messr s
Bunt and Thompson would try and establish the source oft he radioactivity
by paying a visit to Nr . B. Thom• son .
        1 August 1967, 0900 hrs. - Samples from U. F . O. sit e and til.oe e taken
from Michalakbs residence were checked under a u.v. light in the Environ-
mental Sanitation Laboratory in the Norquay Building. The samples taken
f~om the U. F . O. site gave an i ndication that they were contaminated with
Radium luminous paint. The samples from Michalak's house did not res -
pond to the U,V. light . These samples were then packaged andeent to
R.P.D . Ottawa for further analysis.
        1 August 1967 , 1330 h.r s. -A visit was made to Inland Cement
Company where Mr . S. Michalak is employed as an I ndustrial Mechanic .
The purpose ot the visi t wae two fold . Primarily we wanted to rind out

 ~      2 August, 1967, 1400 hrs . - Vi s i ted Mr. B. Thompson at hie re-
sidence at Oakdean Gardens , Suite 312 , 66 Morrow Avenue . He advised us
that he had no eamplee in his suite , but had some at hie mother ' s place
at 28 Frederick E, St . Vital . A series of rook and vegetat i on samp~ee
were checked, but showed no activity, Thompson then produced a soil
sample that he bad obtained from the Falcon Lake U. F . O. site . This sample
proved to be radioactive . Levels up to 1 MR/hr were deteched, Thompson
was moat reluctant to part wi th the sample eo it was left with him . The
sample was sealed in a plastic bag , A contamination che ck was made of
the area where the samples were , using the o.v. light . The area was
extremely cluttered with photographic equipr ent and a great deal of junk .
Several areas responded to the u.v. light, but these did not prove to be
areas of contamination, probably photographic emulsion splashes . Thompson
a ppears to be a very sloppy worker .
        During our conversation with Mr . Thompson it was established that
he worked as a sales representative for a local outdoor advertising firm
(Plaxlab Products Ltd . ) . Hie involvement wi th APRO was a spare time
hobby . He stated that the Winnipeg chapter had approximately fifteen
members , four of whom were inves tigating members . To be an investigating
member , it is necessary to be 21 years or over . He stated that the re-
mainder of the organization was made u~ of teenagers . The APRO organi-
zation appears to be a very loose knit organization . Thompson waa unable
to tell us the names of the other investigating officers, Prior to leaving
Thompson, he advised ue that he had taken some samples to the Nuclear
Medicine Department of the Winnipeg General Hospital . H~ was unwilling
to tell us who had checked the samplee as he said the technician had
carried out a spect ral analysis vnthout hie superior ' s author ity . He
said that one sample showed a 1 .4 mev peak . Two other very weak energy
peaks were also detected . Thompson did not know where the samples were
presently located . These samples had been given to Thompson b,v Michalak .
        3 August , 1967 , 1000 hrs . - A visit was paid to Hart Eleotronioe ,
196~ Osborne  Street . 'rhia visit was arranged at the request of S/L

Defence Chemical Biological and Radiation Lab in Ottawa • It was
forwarded on to our Ot tawa Lab ~ a covering letter from Mr. C.E.
Rubley of DRB on 18 JUL 67 .       4

submitted 8/L Bissky which indicated that Michalak, a l ong with one
Gerald A. V. Hart, 157 Oakview Avenue, East Kildon~ Man. had obtained
the soil sample during a visit to the scene on Sunday , 25 JUN 67 .
According to this report Michalak and Hart recovered the steel tape
referred to by Michalak along with acme burned clothing and obtained
soil samples which were turned over to S/L Bisaky . Miohala.k also
referred to finding a burned circle 15 1 in diameter and had taken
photographs ot this site which he intended to pasa on to S/L Biaaky.

interviewed by a member ot this Force at Winnipeg 1n the presence
of S1L Biasky on 26 JUN 67 at 19:30 hours at which time the above
information was m.a de known to thia Force.

                   ME.MORANDUM FOR FILE

HQ 400-Q-5


                  At 3:25pm on 24 JUL 67, I received a telephone
call from S/Insp. Hldriok of the Ottawa Crime Detection Laboratory
in reference to the ' reported citing of two UFOs by Stefan MI~UK
in the Falcon Lake area of Manitoba on 20 MAY 67, S/Inap, H rick
stated that the Lab had received exhibits in this matter fro the
Defence Research Board including a sample of earth. The exhibits
were then taken over to the Radiation Protection Division, Depart-
ment of Health and Welfare who discovered that the earth saapling
was highly radioactive containing ,J micro-curries of radiation,
He stated that the Department of National Health and Welfare were
concerned over the possibility that others may be contaminated
from this same source and that they intended to send a radiation
expert out to Winnipeg to investigate this matter, Apparently
the thinking at the Radiation Protection Division is that there
ie a possibility that at one time the Manitoba Cancer Clinic may
have dis~oaed of some radioactive material in that area,possibly
MICHALAK baa come acrou this, in which event he could be in
rather bad shape,
2.                The main concern of the Department of National
Health and Welfare is that they d~ not want the pijblio alarmed
but wished to have the area suitably protected to prevent access
by the general public.
3.                 S/Insp . H~~rick stated that he was not fully
conversant with the investigation which our Force had conducted
in this matter , but thought that perhaps a telex should be dispatched
to "D" Division , informing them of the recent findings and closing
off the area concerned to the general public , The question of making
reference to the possibility that the Manitoba Cancer Clinic may have
disposed oJ radioactive material in this area was discussed with
S/Insp. ~~~rick and he considered it advisable to omit this from t he
telex and leave this aspect to the Departmental investigator,
4.                S/Insp . Hidrick pnoned back shortly after to advise
that the name of the depart~ental representative who would be
proceeding to Winnipeg 1s Stuart Hunt and he would be advised to
contact our investigator , Cpl. Davis at Winnipeg on arrival. His
exact date and time oF arrival had not been fixed,

                Stefan MICHALAK - Report of Unidentified Flying Object            2
                Falcon Beach, Man.         20 ~AY 67

 ).           MICHALAK was born inllllllll his bi rthdate is
 He came to Canada in 1949 after serving some period of
 the U. S. Army in the German occupation . He has been employed fo r the
 past six years wit h the Inwand Cement Co . , first in Regina, Sask .,
 and transferred with the company to Winnipeg about 2 years ago . He
 is an industrial mechanic with Inland Cement and enquiries at Inland
 Cement subsequently have revealed that he is a good and trusted employee.
 We have made enquiries at the Selkirk Mental Hospital, Brandon !~e ntal
 Hospital and the Winnipeg Psychiatric Institute to determine whether
 this subject has any history of mental illness or disorders , and a l l
 these enquiries we1·e negative. We have also spoken to persons that
 he associated with on the Friday before this incident and persons that
 he assoc iated with in his line of wor k over t he last few years and
 all consider him mentally stable. None of the people we have interviewed
 had noted any indication of a pending mental disturbance immediately
 prior to his going on the prospecting trip last Friday after noon .
 During the course of conversation with MICHALAK, in the last three days
 he told us that the last person he s poko to before going i nto the bush
 on his prospecting trip was an employee at the Falcon Motel at Falcon
 Beach. He said that he had spoken to this person at about 10:)0 or
 11 :00 o' clock on Friday eveni ng while having a cup of coffee i n the
 Falcon Motel Coffee Shop . I have arranged with members of Falcon
 Beach Detachment to locate and interview this person to further check
 on MICHALAK's mental condition at that time .
 4.           We spoke to him for about 2 hours on Tuesday morning and
 f r om the outset it was obvious that he was very uncomfortable
 physicall y . He said t hat sinoe he had had this experience at Fal con
 Beach on Saturday he had been unable to eat anything and anything he
 t r ied to eat had maae him sick . He claimed to have lost about 13 pounds
 in the three day interval and also said that his head had ached
 severely ever since he had come out of the bush at Falcon Beach . He
 fu r ther sai d that he had a taste in his mouth, a taste that seemed
 to go through his entire system and the taste was like burned wiring
 or insul:.ation and this was bother ing him a great deal. After arriving
 back in Winnipeg on Saturday night by bus he had been taken by his son
 to the Miser~cordi~ Hospital Emerga ncy Dept . and had the burns on his
 abdomen treated . He said that he t old the intern that looked after
 him that the burns had been caused by an air craft exhaust because he
 felt that i1' he told them what really happened he would not be believed
 and they would think that he had lost his mind. He was not admitted to
 the hospita.l , he was treated in the out-patient Emergency Dept . and
 released . Since t he treatment at Miser icordia he has been attended
 by Dr . R. Douglas OATWAY. Dr . OAT~AY has an office at Corydon and
 Lanark i n Winnipeg and is the family doctor for the MICHALAK family .
 Dr . OATWAY has examined him thoroughly and the reason being to try
 and find out why he has lost his appetite and why he is not able
 t9 hold any food that he has tried t o take . He has given him medication
 for his headache and sedatives to try and get him calmed down.
 MICHALAK showed us the burns on his abdomen and chest and there is a
 large burn that covers an area approximately 1 l'uot in diameter. The
 burn when we saw it on Tuesday was blotchy and with unburned areas in-
 side the burned perimeter area. There was no indication ol' blisteri~g.

         7.          When we first spoke to MICHALAK he produced the undershirt he
         had been wearing and said that it had been burned in the fire. He told
         us at this time that his outer shirt had been almost completely destroyed
         by the flash of flame that hit him from the vehicle he claims to have
         seen and he tor e it ot•f at the scene and left it there. He said that he
         threw it down on the ground after he t ook it off and it had started a
         small patch of moss on fire and he tramped this out and left the shirt
         at the spot . He put the fire on his undershirt out a nd put that in his
         leather briefcase and brou~ht it back with hi.m. Cst. ZACHARIAS and I
         examined the undershirt ana smilled it and it smelled like burned
         electrica~ wiring or insulation to both of us .   He was wearing a nylon
         ski-type cap at the time he encountered this object in the bush and was
         also wearing gri nder's goggl es to chip rock, to protect his eyes. He
         had his cap on backwards and the front part of his cap (the part facing
         the front which would normally be at the back) was melted in a small
         area , obviously from heat . He was also wearing a pair of yellow plastic
         gloves which have a clo~h interior and are covered with plastic on the
         ou~side .  Mr. THOMPSON, the APRO man, took one of these gloves, the one
         which was damaged on the fingers where he claims he touched the vehicle .
         We did not discover that THQ1.1PSON had this glove until he departed with
         it and we have not recovered it from him . Mr . MICHALAK claims that t1,.0
         or three fingers on the glove were damaged when he touched the vehicle .
         His undershirt and his cap are still i .n the possession of the people at
         the Manitoba Cancer Research Clinic .
         8.          We spoke to MICHALAK on Tuesday, Wednesday and also Thursday
         of this week hoping that he would feel fit to ac company us down to the

         improving at all and up until Thursday he had still not taken any solid
         foods since his experience . At our request Dr. OATwAY ex~~i ned him
         again Thursday morning to see if he felt he should make the trip down there
         and Dr . OATNAY decided at that time he should not go, at least n¢t at

         not successful in finding any indication of a landing spot . We then put
         down on the Falcon Lake Golf Course and examined the most likely a rea ,
         in the area indicated by MICHALAK , on foot. During our talk with him

           De&r Sir1
                       Thank you for the report or the bright meteor or
           September 17, originally telephoned to me, and oleo contained
           in your letter or 19 September, your No. lt:l-400-Q-5.
                       Tho collection of reports duri~ the past tew days
           strongly suggests that the meteor ended over !..aka Huron. The
           disappearance of tho object behind local hills or trees while
           still bright enough to light up the sky, caused many· observers
           to conclude the object had fallen nearby and perhaps started
           a fire, Por moat of these observers, the meteor was at leaot
           50 to 100 111iles away. Clo:~er to Lake Huron, the reports generall7
           mention loud explosions which are typical tor the region•
           within about 50 miles or a meteorite fall,
                                             Yours sincerely,
